" <p> Mr Barter said late summer warmth and light gave plants a chance to get ready for winter , with wood and buds ripening fully and becoming more resistant to cold damage . <p> The cooler , wet conditions of the summer had led to diseases such as downy mildews and potato blight , but these had largely dried up due to the higher temperatures and dry air . 
@@4353177 <h> 02 October 2011 11:47 AM <h> British Gas Customer Relations : the dumbest organisation in the world ? <p> Unsolicited marketing calls are bad . Unsolicited marketing calls by machines are the work of Beelzebub . But getting phoned by a machine which tells you it is too busy to take your call is so grim it is almost funny . I can not think of a better example of technology being used quite so ineptly and think it is about time that a bit of publicity was given to the sheer , almost malicious incompetence of a firm that is surely big enough to know better . <p> For the past few weeks we have been getting mysterious , unsolicited calls from an automated system that claims to be from British Gas . These calls usually come at just the time my son is being put to bed or when one of us is putting the dinner on . Annoying enough . But it is what the ' caller ' says that is so surreal . <p> " This is a call from British Gas , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at this point . But curiosity has got the better of us . " You do not need to worry , " the recording continues , unnecessarily . We are not worried ; cross , a bit , but worried , no . Now the good bit . The recording drones on : " We are unable to allocate an adviser to take your call at the moment ! " <p> Great . The dinner is burning and I just been summoned to the phone to be told a bloody machine ca n't be bothered to speak to me . The message goes on to suggest that if I do not wish to get these calls I have to call another number . <p> I do so , to be met by another machine ( perhaps these are people in a call centre PRETENDING to be machines , having a laugh ) telling me I will have to provide it with all sorts of details such as addresses , account numbers and so on . At no point , incidentally , has anyone , or anything , actually attempted to sell @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ here . <p> I smell a rat and put the phone down . This could be some sort of ' phishing ' exercise , I think , whereby scamsters pretend to be respectable companies , particularly banks or utilities , in order to inveigle passwords and other secret data from their unsuspecting victims . Or it could be an outfit building up data with the aim of creating stolen identities . <p> Get details of my British Gas bills and put it together with my address , phone number and so on and that would be a useful part of the jigsaw for anyone who wanted to use my name to open a fake account or get a passport . <p> After a bit of Googling I discover that I am not alone . Whoever is doing this is pretending to be British Gas , it seems , and is phoning a LOT of people . You 'd better warn your customers about this . " She agrees and promises to investigate . <p> But here 's the thing , the extraordinary thing . The by-now sheepish press officer admits that the calls ARE being made by British Gas , a gigantic national utility worth billions yet which has clearly decided to spend 75p on the world 's most incompetent automated telemarketing software . This is a system which could almost be designed to alienate its customers and make sure they have nothing to do with the wretched firm ever again -- to the extent that this customer even thought he was being targeted by criminals . <p> The company 's explanation , such as it was revolved around the fact that they are trying to sell me more insurance for my boiler , or somesuch ( why @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ statement said : " We tried to contact Mr Hanlon to offer him a potential additional facility for his existing HomeCare agreement . There are occasions when a call is made but for some reasons no one is able to take the call , and unfortunately this occurred in two out of three calls to Mr Hanlon this week which is highly unusual . <p> " In such cases , we are required under OFCOM rules to leave a message , explaining that there is no one to take the call and we are also required to give customers the option to opt out of future calls which is why we ask for the customer 's account details . " <p> ' OFCOM rules ' eh ? Along with ' health &amp; safety ' ' in the interests of security ' and the ' data protection act ' these are only ever ( spuriously ) quoted when a company is trying to find a way of excusing bad or incompetent behaviour or simply can not be bothered to deal with you . Apparently what happened to us occurs in 2% of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ under the impression that there is some sort of recession on . It beggars belief that a company like this can afford to alienate one in 50 of its customers by harassing them with nonsensical phone calls that must leave quite a few folk worried that they are being targeted by fraudsters . I can not really object to a company trying to drum up business , but not like this . <p> In the old days , the nationalised utilities were hardly a byword for slickness and efficiency but at least they did not generally harass you . What really annoys me is the knowledge that people so blitheringly incompetent have been given positions of power in such a huge firm . <p> Does this happen in Sweden ? In Germany ? I doubt it . @@4353203 <h> Why protecting the world 's wildlife is good for our wallets <p> A new world body on wildlife and ecosystems protection being set up by the UN must avoid blaming developing nations , where most of the world 's biodiversity loss is occurring , says a top British scientist . <p> Overconsumption by rich western nations is as big a driver of global environmental degradation as the rapidly growing populations of developing countries , says Professor Bob Watson , a leading figure in setting up the Intergovernmental Platform on Biodiversity and Ecosystem Services ( IPBES ) . <p> The new body -- modelled on the UN 's Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change ( IPCC ) -- will assess how and why the natural world is being degraded , what it will cost society , and what can be done to halt the process . <p> But it must avoid rows between rich and poor countries , says Professor Watson , an ex-head of the IPCC , who is Chief Scientific Adviser to Britain 's Department for Environment , Food and Rural Affairs . " If they think @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , telling them what to do , that 'll be the end of it ... The climate debate has been , ' you rich countries got rich by using cheap fossil fuels , and now you 're telling us not to use them . ' We must not get into that , " he said . Regional assessments of biodiversity problems must be " owned " by the regions concerned , he said . So if there is a regional biodiversity assessment of Latin America , scientists from Latin America will carry it out , not foreign scientists . <p> Professor Watson will play a key role at a Nairobi meeting today which will decide how the new body can be formed , probably next year . Hopes are high that the IPBES might help halt the loss of global wildlife and habitats . <p> The IPBES is based on the increasingly influential concept of ecosystem services , that forests rivers or peat bogs are not just parts of the natural world , but produce oxygen , provide food and store atmospheric carbon , vital in the fight against climate change @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ global nations back , follows on the heels of two reports : the Millennium Ecosystems Assessment of 2005 , which showed that most of the world 's ecosystems are in serious decline , and the report on The Economics of Ecosystems and Biodiversity released last year , which estimated that nature and the services it provides are worth trillions of dollars annually to society . <p> The IPBES will aim to show the value of biodiversity both in ethical or social terms , and in economic terms . <p> Professor Watson is seen as the ideal person to oversee the UN 's biodiversity body , as he has led international environmental assessments of all major global concerns : ozone depletion , climate change , ecosystem change and agriculture and development . <p> In fact , the 63-year-old atmospheric chemist , from Romford , east London , who still speaks with an East End accent , is the world 's leading authority on policy responses to global change . Yet he is far from being a household name in Britain since he spent 34 years of his career in the US , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ White House , and the World Bank . He chaired the IPCC from 1997 to 2002 . <p> He says global ecosystems face a " headlong assault " from five drivers -- land conversion ( such as deforestation ) , over-exploitation ( such as overfishing ) , the introduction of exotic species , pollution , and climate change . <p> And he does not think climate change can be stopped at a rise of two degrees Celsius , which is the goal of most world climate policy . " We had better be prepared to adapt to four degrees , " the professor commented . <p> What the ecosystem is worth <p> Ask yourself what human society gets from a forest and the obvious answer is wood . Another obvious answer might be wildlife . Or perhaps , a pleasurable stroll . But that does n't begin to list the benefits provided to us by a great aggregation of trees . <p> Forests such as the Amazon are ecosystems which provide the world with tremendous services that are essential to the continuance of human life . These include vast amounts of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , as well as the storing of billions of tonnes of the carbon dioxide which human industry is pumping into the atmosphere and which is causing the world to heat up , with potentially disastrous consequences . And at last , the real value of these ecosystem services is being realised . <p> Last year the UN released a ground-breaking report on The Economics of Ecosystems and Biodiversity which put monetary value on the benefits the natural world provides us with . It runs into trillions of dollars annually , the report said . <p> For example , it suggested that the value of human welfare benefits provided by coral reefs was up to ? 109bn annually . The destruction of coral reefs is not only damaging to marine life but also poses risks to communities , the report said . Some 30 million people around the world rely on reef-based resources for food production , and for their livelihoods . <p> In another example , the report said that the economic value of insect pollinators , such as honey bees , in global crop production was ? 134bn a year @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and grasslands was valued at between $2trn and $4.5trn annually . But these figures are not included in economic data such as GDP , or in corporate accounts . <p> Now the hope is that with the IPBES , they will be taken into account , and a true picture of how much biodiversity loss is costing the world will emerge .

@@4354547 <h> The Melancholia of the middle classes <p> Lars von Trier 's new film brilliantly teases out the link between the rot of the bourgeois mind and the rise of apocalyptic fantasies . <p> I <p> 3 October 2011 <h> Get spiked by email <p> It is an unwritten law of modern moviemaking that the more realistic-looking the apocalypse becomes , the less believable it is . Films like The Day After Tomorrow and 2012 may have bombarded our eyes with a CGI-fuelled glimpse into the end of days , but they were far too daft to make an impact on our hearts and minds . Never had watching the obliteration of mankind felt so tepidly unmoving . <p> Melancholia , directed by Dogme badboy turned darling of the European arthouse , Lars von Trier , is different . It eschews the big-bucks bulldozing of major cities in favour of showing the apocalypse play out amongst a dysfunctional upper middle-class family in a remote mansion in some unnamed country . And , as bizarre as it may sound , the end result is a properly gripping drama , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , about our End Times obsession , than any Hollywood film has managed . <p> The genius of Melancholia is that it draws a direct link between the soullessness of the modern bourgeois existence and the flourishing of fantasies about the end of the world . Indeed , the apocalypse in the movie is inseparable from the existential disarray of its assorted vulgar characters . It seems almost to be a physical manifestation of the intellectual and bodily lethargy of the lead character in particular - Justine ( Kirsten Dunst ) , a pretty , well-off , cushily employed woman who has just got hitched to a man who looks like one of the hunks from True Blood ( Alexander Skarsg ? rd ) , yet who is inexplicably unhappy . As everyone keeps saying to her , with increasing levels of bemusement , ' You should be happy ' . <p> In Part 1 of the film , titled ' Justine ' , we watch as Justine survives being the bride in the bourgeois wedding from hell . Skating perilously close to parody , von Trier gives us a mad @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ dad , for example , played by John Hurt , is obsessed with buxom women called Betty . It 's hard to work out who 's the most obnoxious character : Justine ; her mother , played by Charlotte Rampling , who is so wantonly icy she makes the Ice Queen of Narnia look like St Bernadette ; or Justine 's brother-in-law ( Kiefer Sutherland ) , who I think is meant to symbolise Capitalism , because all he talks about his how much filthy lucre he has . <p> It is during Justine 's loveless wedding that we first see Melancholia , a planet that has been hidden behind the sun for eons but which is now on the move . At first , the characters mistake the faint red entity in the nighttime sky for a star , but as the film progresses we discover that it is in fact a planet , vastly bigger than Earth , and heading our way . It 's no coincidence that this celestial object makes itself visible on the evening of Justine 's nuptials - because its progress , even the name @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ state of mind . Just as Justine 's loss of joie de vivre is puzzling , so is the sudden appearance of this long-lost gigantic orb . <h> Related categories <p> Part 2 of the film , titled ' Claire ' , focuses on Justine 's sister ( Charlotte Gainsbourg ) . A smart and optimistic sort , and the mother of a young son , Claire wants to live , and thus sees the potential collision between Melancholia and Earth as something terrible . Justine , by contrast , welcomes it , even discovering a new lease of life , one perversely based on her fervent hope that all life might soon be extinguished . ' Life on Earth is evil ' , she tells Claire , as casually as ... well , as casually as your average environmentalist will these days denounce the hubris of humankind . Melancholia seems to grow or shrink , move closer to or farther from Earth , depending on whose mood is stronger : hopeful Claire 's or bitter Justine 's . <p> In intimately tying up End Times with the moodiness of a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and seems to be developing ME ( that 's what her lethargy mostly brings to mind ) , von Trier taps into a profound truth about modern-day apocalyptic miserabilism : its origins lie less in real likely events than in the rot of the bourgeois mind and body politic . <p> All of today 's frequent fretting about the demise of days - or ' global warming ' , ' peak oil ' , ' the tipping point ' , to give it some of its deceptively secularist monikers - likewise springs from the existential crises of influential sections of society . It is decadent , self-pitying bourgeois thinkers and activists , middle-class miserabilists , real-life Justines , who have fashioned the idea that we are heading towards certain doom , and in every single instance it is their own inner turmoil that has led them to embrace such fiery fantasies . Just as Melancholia seems to bulge and hum in tune with Justine 's moral self-immolation , so today 's warnings about climatic catastrophe always reveal tonnes more about campaigners ' narcissistic angst than they do about what will happen @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Sometimes , the sons and daughters of the well-off and well-connected will unwittingly let slip that their apocalypitis is All About Them . So the unfathomably wealthy green David de Rothschild says he first got serious about climate-change campaigning during a jolly to the North Pole , when ' I felt like nothing more than a speck of dust on the endless horizon ' . Franny Armstrong , director of the green ' documentary ' The Age of Stupid , admits that for someone like her , ' a member of the MTV generation ' , it 's almost a relief that climate change has helped to make life ' so much more meaningful than what was planned ' . Recently , a bevy of implacably middle-class young hacks were taken on a freebie trip to the Arctic by a green group , and all of them dutifully filed newspaper columns about their Damascene conversions to green apocalypse-fretting , because ' there 's nothing like a glacier crumbling into the sea in front of your eyes ' to remind you that the end of the world is nigh , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ planet of Melancholia seems to mould itself around Justine 's moods , so today 's climate-change hysteria always seems to encapsulate at-sea rich kids ' feeling that life is losing its point and their view of people ( Other People , that is ) as dirty and dangerous . In one of the more controversial scenes in the movie , Justine writhes naked and with undisguised glee in the nighttime light cast by Melancholia upon Earth . But is that really such a weird image at a time when , day in , day out , we 're snowballed with eco-porn about coming floods and storms of locusts written by people who clearly get some kind of kick from doom-dredging ? It is tres amusant that Europe 's cultural elite is heaping praise upon von Trier 's film ( even if much of it is deserved ) , because this movie looks to me like a sometimes stinging exploration of the cultural elite 's own superbly narcissistic habit of magicking up ' coming apocalypses ' which always seem perfectly to reflect their own fears and prejudices and desperation for some Day @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The final 10 minutes of Melancholia contain some of the best special effects I 've ever seen , way more subtle yet far more believable than the end-of-world fare dished up in apocalyporno films like 2012 . The intense noise and oppressive growth of Melancholia ( the cinema I was in felt like it was shaking ) will nearly convince you that life as we know it has just ended - and that it 's all down to a bourgeois princess 's desire for doom , her loathing of ' life on Earth ' .

@@4356440 <h> Interview with Deborah Baker , director of people at Sky <p> No-one working in the media could fail to be impressed by the brand personality Sky exudes and I was no exception when I visited Sky 's director of people , Deborah Baker , last month . <p> I fell into the excitement I enjoyed , on work experience as a student , looking around editing suites and buzzing newsrooms , seeing film crews and reporters heading off to break an exclusive . <p> Making news as much as it is breaking news , the broadcasting giant has come a long way from its humble origins as Sky TV in 1989 . After merging with British Satellite Broadcasting in 1990 , it became British Sky Broadcasting Group . Commonly known as BskyB , but trading as Sky , it is the largest pay-TV broadcaster in the UK , with more than 10 million subscribers . <p> And , while walking around the Sky HQ near Isleworth in West London , it is hard to believe the small town that makes up its studios and offices was , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in an industrial estate . Some of the estate warehouses and outlets remain dotted about the complex , as the TV behemoth engulfs it . <p> But Sky has been hitting the headlines itself , as media mogul Rupert Murdoch spent most of 2010 and 2011 attempting to increase his company News Corporation 's 39.1% stake in Sky to 100% . He withdrew in July this year . <p> HR magazine mused as to whether staff at Sky would have breathed a sigh of relief , contrasting the cultures of the apparently unethical News International , with Sky 's transparent , accountable and ' fun ' work environment . <p> When I repeat it to her , Baker - ever the corporate diplomat - thinks carefully before she responds to the statement . <p> " People here are very confident about Sky . The bid happened , but now it 's business as usual for us . It always was , " she explains . " Staff were aware of what was going on - but the people who work here know what Sky is about . They are comfortable with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ own view on the proposals ? This time she answers immediately . " We are our own plc and we did n't have anything to do with the bid , " she explains . " If I were HR director at News Corporation , I would have certainly been involved , though . " <p> Addressing the idea of cultures , Baker admits she ca n't comment on News Corp , but waxes lyrical about Sky : " It was the people that attracted me to come and work here . The job is not irrelevant , but I would put the people first and at Sky I know our people are passionate about what they do . It 's not just about the individual here . It 's about the team . " She pauses , then smiles : " Sky 's tagline is ' believe in better ' and we do n't just use it on our advertising . It is our value for staff . I have to admit that when I joined Sky , I thought , ' what 's all this about ? ' But the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ realise it is about what we do : we want our staff to achieve greater things , we want to improve - not just our company , but our environment . I hate HR jargon - so just give me simple-speak such as this . " <p> With a history of working in fashion ( Burberry and Laura Ashley are on the CV ) , it is no surprise Baker is chic and stylish . In her office , it is impossible to miss a large photograph of another style icon , Audrey Hepburn , posing with a cigarette ? la Breakfast at Tiffany 's . <p> Emblazoned across the image , it reads : ' Believe in better . ' So it is safe to assume Baker is now on board with the slogan . <p> The reward programmes work to incentivise ideas of ' being better ' and teamwork , and engage staff with what Sky is moving towards . <p> Engagement is a priority , says Baker . Its employee engagement and benefits programmes include free Sky TV , broadband and telephone usage for all employees ( working @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ plan into which the company contributes 8% worth of staff salary.Staff also have access to an exclusive ' Sky Insider ' facility , where they can view shows weeks before they are available on other Sky channels . <p> Baker is emphatic on the importance of engagement in the business and transparent discussions with employees . " We measure levels of employee engagement in our people survey - and we are taking the results to benchmark against high-performing organisations . At the end of the surveys , we ask staff to tell us one great thing about working at Sky - and also something they would like to change . <p> " This year , we had 22,000 comments from 16,500 staff - and I want to hear them . It is always useful to have this open discussion . I have a bi-weekly meeting with the CEO and I talk about these issues - it helps us prepare our plans for what might happen in the industry . " But ' the industry ' means different things to different people . For some , such as me , it is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , or films ; and for yet others , Sky is about home broadband or telecommunications . <p> Company performance is n't too shabby . Annual results to 30 June 2011 , showed a reported profit after tax of ? 896 million - its highest ever . @@4356575 <h> Climate swings increase extinction risk <p> Image caption Species threatened by climate change can be relocated , but run the risk of becoming invasive , like the American bullfrog <p> Scientists have begun to predict the animals that may become extinct in the next century because of climate change . <p> Researchers at Brown University in the US have combined predictions of climate change with the geographic ranges of well-studied amphibians . <p> While the animals will try to migrate to areas with more suitable weather , short-term temperature fluctuations can cut them off . <p> The findings suggest more effort should be made to relocate vulnerable species . <p> It has been recognised for the past decade that the continuing future trend of global warming may drive species to permanently migrate in order to stay in an ideal habitat . <p> Amid concerns that this long-term migration may be disrupted by towns and cities , scientists at Brown University Dr Regan Early and Prof Dov Sax set out to predict the shifts in species ' ranges over the next century . <p> Predictions of global climate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ oscillations and local climatic effects will play a role for given species . <p> The researchers combined these climate models with information on the ranges and tolerances of various species of frogs , toads and salamanders in the western US , with results that " really surprised " , said Dr Early . <p> While they set out to find the disrupting effect of urban areas , they instead saw that the short-term climate fluctuations were enough to stop a species ' migration in its tracks , cutting it off from ideal habitats and driving it to extinction . <h> Physical tolerance <p> Fifteen species of amphibians native to the western US were modelled in the study , as their ranges are well-known and their tolerances to physical extremes have been well-studied . <p> Image caption The California newt will struggle to migrate into a new habitat because of climate fluctuations <p> While none of these species is currently at risk , they predicted that over half of them would become extinct or endangered in the next 100 years due to these climate fluctuations . <p> Among the factors determining whether @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ can migrate and its persistence , or robustness , in the face of climatic change . <p> For example , the models suggested that the Foothill Yellow-Legged frog would be able to make it into a new area , despite climate fluctuations , while the California newt would not fare so well in its migration across the Californian Central Valley . <p> Dr Early said : " This species is n't endangered now , and in the future there is more than enough suitable habitat for it to remain safe , but ... the newt has a really hard time following its climate path to its future range because repeated climatic fluctuations cause it to retreat over and over again . " <p> The tolerance of an animal to less-than-ideal climatic conditions will determine whether it can survive long enough to complete its migration . <p> " There is a lot of uncertainty in the ability of species to persist , and this is an under-appreciated factor , " said Dr Early . <p> " For example , if an animal lives for a long time , it may fare better @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ n't survive one year , being able to lay again the following year will increase the chances of survival . " <h> Managed relocation <p> While the study was carried out on only a few species in a limited geographic range , the researchers are confident that the global climate fluctuations will drive similar patterns all around the world . <p> Small mammals , insects and plants are expected to react in a way similar to the amphibians , as they have similar tolerances to climate change ; larger mammals may be less affected as their habitats are less climate-specific , the researchers believe . <p> Image caption Climate change could put the speckled black salamander into new areas beyond its current reach <p> The findings from this research are expected to add some clarity to discussions on whether to actively relocate species at risk from climate change . <p> There is concern over this " managed relocation " from conservation groups and governmental organisations , as the reactions of an ecosystem to the sudden introduction of a non-native species are poorly understood . <p> But research published in the journal @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ loss could result in the extinction of 15-37% of all species , lends support to the idea of managed relocation as a way of maintaining biodiversity . <p> This new study from Dr Early and Prof Sax highlights the specific risks to species from climate fluctuations cutting off their migration paths . <p> " There are a lot of species that wo n't be able to take care of themselves , " Prof Sax said . " We may instead need to consider using managed relocation more frequently than has been previously considered . "

Instead of Hank Williams , Bono was now listening to the industrial racket of The Young Gods and Einst ? rzende Neubauten . @@4365421 <h> The importance of creating and keeping a customer <p> " The purpose of business is to create and keep a customer , " Peter Drucker , the legendary management guru , once said . No matter how de-layered , outsourced , virtualised or re-imagined businesses become , the company of the future will still need to remain focused on this simple fact . <p> Companies are formed for a reason . Sole traders are limited in what they can produce , and , if they are any good , unable to satisfy the growing demand for their services . So individuals -- entrepreneurs -- become employers , and companies grow . <h> More <h> IN Future of the Company <p> Size then presents its own difficulties . Bureaucracy develops . Processes evolve and become embedded . Sometimes these processes seem hard to change even though circumstances demand that they do . Attitudes can become frozen . Tom Peters , the management writer , has suggested , cheekily , that most big businesses are actually dying -- they just do not realise it yet . The creative destruction @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and will see off any organisation that stands still for too long . <p> The company of the future may perhaps be saved by its customers -- if it pays enough attention to them . When Mr Drucker was asked to talk to General Motors ' senior management team almost a decade ago , this was the thrust of his argument . <p> Speaking to the assembled corporate leaders from his California home via a squawk box on the table in Detroit , he asked a simple , but deadly , question : " Why should I buy a GM car ? " <p> Several answers were forthcoming but , as one eye-witness to the meeting recalled : " Nobody actually had a good answer to the question ! " It should have been clear back then that GM was heading for trouble . Customers should remain agenda item number one . <p> Senior executives and strategists worry a lot about whether they have the right business model to make sure they will hold on to ( and acquire more ) customers , thus giving their company a future . But @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ entrepreneurship at London Business School , they would do well to consider what their " management model " is too . The company of the future will need to take a view on that . Companies are made up of people , and their work has to be managed in one way or another . <p> But , argue Ed Lawler , Chris Worley and David Creelman in their recent book , Management Reset -- Organizing For Sustainable Effectiveness , to understand the future of management you have to understand the past . <p> The authors set out a clear three-part summary of the history ( and future ) of management in their narrative . Stage one of modern management was the era of command-and-control organisations : the marriage of bureaucracy with " the scaleable technology of mass production " , they write . Henry Ford 's revolutionary production line remains the best-known symbol of this period . <p> Improved education and training of staff , allied to the increased sophistication of the work involved , led to their second era , that of " high-involvement organisations " . In this @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as employees ' views and experiences had to be taken into account . <p> But the authors suggest that a " management reset " is now necessary for three reasons . First , previous approaches " wrongly assume that the business environment will be relatively stable . " They continue : " Buried deep in the managerial psyche is the belief that change is the enemy and that financial success can best be achieved by remaining stable . " <p> Second , thanks to rapid globalisation , the world for which " command and control " and " high involvement " businesses were constructed no longer exists . Businesses need to become far more flexible and adaptive . And third , the overwhelming evidence of climate change and environmental degradation requires management to be rethought , they say . <p> The goal now , for the company of the future , is to become a " sustainable management organisation " . SMOs are different . They set themselves up to be more flexible and fleet of foot than their competitors . They reject a stolid strategy development process in favour of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ by drawing on the insights and experiences of employees at all levels in the business . Work is organised differently too : to a great extent it is controlled by employees and customers rather than tiers of management . And talented employees lie at the heart of the SMO : not being promised jobs for life , but a deal which makes sense to both sides . <p> Which SMOs do the authors like the look of ? Cisco , the technology company , has ripped up traditional organisational charts to try and construct a business that can respond to the future demands of the market . PepsiCo , the drinks and snacks group , has taken on board sustainability as an idea that should inform management decisions and product development . The remarkably flat , almost non-existent hierarchy at W.L. Gore , the maker of Gore-Tex fabric , means that ideas have to live or die by the support they win internally from a highly intelligent and sceptical workforce . All these businesses are trying to build a healthier future for themselves . <p> Perhaps most radically of all , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has chosen to ignore decades of human resources orthodoxy to introduce its own highly pragmatic approach to employing people : a one-year renewable contract for all . " Adequate performance gets you a good severance package " is the watchword . <p> Perhaps the company has been too smart for its own good , however . Its recent decision to bump up its subscription rates dramatically -- in an attempt to move more customers to the online-only service -- backfired badly , losing the company 800,000 subscribers . Sometimes , it seems , you can be too radical . <p> The future of the company may be uncertain , but not everything will change . Keeping customers happy will continue to be the chief task of business -- just as Mr Drucker suggested . <p> The author is visiting professor of management practice at Cass Business School <p> Copyright The Financial Times Limited 2015 . You may share using our article tools . Please do n't cut articles from FT.com and redistribute by email or post to the web . 
@@4365497 <h> Flood risk -- the impact on lenders <p> Home owners vulnerable to flood risk may be unable get insurance , sell or remortgage their property after June 2013 . James Sherwood-Rogers , chair of the Know Your Flood Risk campaign , discusses what both homeowners and mortgage lenders need to be aware of in the run-up to this insurance deadline <p> Unpredictable weather patterns are among the many factors as to why we are seeing an increase in the levels of flood risk across the UK . The events at Boscastle in 2004 , Carlisle in 2005 , Hull in 2007 and Cockermouth in 2009 offer four very stark and memorable reminders of the dangers that flood water can bring to residential communities . And while these are particularly extreme cases , there is no doubt that flooding of any scale is an increasing threat with one in four homes considered to be ' at risk ' of flooding* . <p> Flooding has dominated the news agenda over recent years with individual 's personal stories , remarkable rescues and the subsequent high costs to both consumers and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at the 2007 floods for example , the Environment Agency ( EA ) reported that the average cost to affected households was between ? 23,000 and ? 30,000 per flooded home , which does n't take into account future ' saleability ' and any impact this may have on a property 's value . <p> The Association of British Insurers ( ABI ) released its Quarterly Consumer Survey 2011 in June , in which it reported that the average value of insurance claims for a flood in 2010 was over ? 12,000 per claim , far outweighing claim values for other threats including fire , subsidence , theft , storms and burst pipes , much to the respondents ' surprise where 51 per cent wrongly believed that fire would instead prove to be the most expensive claim . <p> And , while traditionally the insurance industry has covered the cost of repairs and replacement when flooding occurs , changes are afoot in the insurance world that may not only impact consumers , but has the potential to create some unease across the mortgage lending industry too . <p> Important deadline <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and the Government commits insurers to continue to provide flood insurance under certain scenarios . This SoP comes to an end on 30 June 2013 leaving an uncertain future for both homeowners and lenders who may be exposed by future flood risk . <p> When looking in closer detail at the SoP , it states that until 30 June 2013 , ABI members are committed to continuing to make flood insurance for domestic properties and small businesses available as a feature of standard insurance policies . This is based on the flood risk being classed as ' not significant ' , which in general is defined as no worse than a 1.3 per cent or one in 75 annual probability of flooding . <p> Furthermore , insurers will continue to offer flood cover to existing domestic property and small business customers at significant flood risk providing the Executive has announced plans and notified the ABI of its intention to reduce the risk for those customers below significant within five years . Despite this however , it is important to note that the premiums charged and policy terms will reflect the level @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Also , this commitment does not apply to any new property that was built after 1 January 2009 . <p> With the end of the SoP , the future for insuring against flood is uncertain , leaving home and small business owners potentially exposed . As matters currently stand , as of 30 June 2013 , indications are that vulnerable home owners may be unable to sell or remortgage their property . And , with the deadline fast approaching and flood defence budgets not set to increase in the near future , what do both homeowners and mortgage lenders need to be aware of in the run-up to June 2013 ? <p> The impact on mortgage lenders <p> It is a standard condition that for all mortgages , the property in question must be covered by buildings insurance , including flood cover , for the term of the loan in order to protect both the borrower and the mortgage lender and mitigate them both from any potential risks that affect that property . <p> If we are faced with the situation that ABI members withdraw their cover from both existing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is a lack of support in helping mitigate flood risk , the worst-case-scenario would be that mortgages could be made invalid , making thousands of property 's un-mortgageable with the values likely to be impacted as a result . And , if we take into account that most insurance policies run for a 12-month period , this brings forward the deadline industry must consider to 1 July 2012 -- just over half a year away . <p> Currently , flood risk is not automatically included in the mortgage application , valuation or conveyancing process as standard . Perhaps providing knowledgeable data on such risk factors could help support lenders with their existing due diligence processes ? The benefits of feeding accurate flood-risk information directly into the existing mortgage valuation process could deliver additional intelligence on which to form the basis of an accurate mortgage offer . <p> The challenge then falls at sourcing data in a usable way and from reliable sources . To date , no nationally-agreed flood map has been developed for the UK . The EA does n't currently cover surface water ( pluvial ) flooding in its @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ floods , 50 per cent of the flooding occurred away from designated floodplains , with 60-70 per cent specifically due to pluvial flooding . Therefore , the need for such centralised data is high . <p> Assumptions on whether a property is at risk can not be made simply by looking at postcodes or whether it 's near the sea or a river , and so a centralised , nationally-agreed flood map would certainly help not only consumers in understanding their exposure to risk , but also provide additional intelligence to mortgage lenders and insurers . <p> By feeding such data into the mortgage lending process , it may help lending organisations avoid getting ' caught out ' by a property 's unknown history or non-visible risks . The value of this is high , as ultimately if lenders ' security comes to be worth far less than expected , exposure to risk is increased , particularly if the borrower defaults on payments and the lender has to subsequently reclaim the security . <p> This is , of course , only looking at new mortgage applications . But what about the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ will need to happen in order to understand the potential levels of exposure that lenders may face ? If insurance becomes unavailable for the most serious high risk properties , what will this do to the value as , effectively , the properties may become un-saleable ? And , if this is the case , what is the future of house prices in places such as Hull or Cockermouth ? <p> There are certainly a lot more questions than answers at this stage . I do n't believe mortgage lenders have really grasped the nettle regarding this issue and , as the deadline fast approaches , many decisions need to be made in terms of calculating the potential risk to the industry , and to determine what activities need to happen before 1 July 2012 in order to safeguard assets and come up with a strategy that suits all parties involved . <p> So , what next ? <p> With approximately 200,000 homes falling into the serious ' at risk ' bracket , which equates to a greater than 75-1 chance of flooding , the change in insurances could have a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ risk continuing to increase we could see this figure rise , particularly in light of unpredictable weather conditions via climate change and an increasing number of properties being built on regenerated brownfield or low-level ground that may already fall within existing EA flood plains . <p> As the insurance rules change , lenders must look at ways of not only identifying the potential risk to their organisation but to work with consumers , industry and government at how those at a higher risk of flood can be protected in future to secure their investments . <p> Without doing this , there is a potential knock-on effect that could include a significant drop in the value of affected properties therefore making the security worth much less than the amount borrowed . Or , borrowers fail to renew building insurance due to expensive premiums or excesses , inadvertently invalidating the mortgage agreement . The latter of which will require much policing from lenders to ensure appropriate insurance is in place throughout the term of the mortgage . <p> Perhaps the existing application , valuation or conveyancing process needs to be updated to incorporate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ work more closely with insurers to understand risk -- as well as recognise which properties have invested in flood defences . <p> Whatever changes are afoot , the industry must work with government and the ABI to identify a way in which properties with a greater risk of flood are covered . It 's in the interest of all parties involved to ensure appropriate protection is in place . <p> Flood Risk Strategy Round Table <p> The Know Your Flood Risk campaign team is planning to host a strategy session in London during November with mortgage lenders and flood industry experts and associations to discuss the forthcoming Statement of Principles deadline and to agree specific actions that need to take place before 1 July 2012 . Invitations will be issued to industry representatives soon , however to express an interest in attending the round table , email **28;207;TOOLONG . <p> *The calculation of one in four British homes at flood risk is based on the number of residential homes within the 2008 RMS 1,000-year Inland Flood risk zones compared to the total number of homes in Britain . The flood risk @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ flooding including flooding from major and minor rivers and surface water flow . The 1,000-year flood risk map identifies areas that would be at risk under very extreme rainfall and flooding . <p> Insurance banding for flood risk <p> As outlined in the latest RICS Flooding guide for property owners , insurance companies grade flood risk to properties in three bands based on the likelihood of flooding to occur in any given year : <p> Band 1 = classifies the risk at up to 200 to 1 chance of a flood , annually <p> Band 2 = classifies the risk to be between 200-1 and 75-1 chance , annually <p> Band 3 = classifies as the most serious risk , with a greater than 75-1 chance of flood , annually <p> As a result of the change to the SoP on 30 June 2013 , the ABI will no longer guarantee automatic flood risk insurance to properties in band three , although this is subject to review . <p> Personal experience <p> When Mary Dhonau OBE , chief executive of the Know Your Flood Risk campaign , bought her house in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , as it was not on an officially designated flood plain . <p> However , her home was repeatedly flooded with raw sewage when the drains were overwhelmed , following heavy rainfall . The area had an 80 year history of being flooded . <p> Had Mary had that information before purchasing her home , she would have been able to make an informed decision and either chosen to walk away from the purchase or looked into the potential of protecting her home using Kitemarked flood protection products . <p> Mary Dhonau said : " Owner-occupiers suffer the devastation to their home and belongings and the immense disturbance to their lives that flood can cause . The average cost for repairing a flood-damaged house is about ? 30,000 . <p> " The cost of insurance has risen enormously as a result , and affected homeowners have been saddled with huge increases in excesses . Most people are just unable to afford such huge price tags and the future for them looks bleak . <p> " When the Statement of principles runs out in 2013 , many home owners may find themselves @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ situation is totally unacceptable and moves must be taken to support people who face the risk of flooding . <p> " Over the last decade or so , the UK has experienced an increase in frequency of heavy rainfall which leads to surface water flooding and , as a result , we have seen a high number of catastrophic floods in recent years . Surface water flooding can affect all homes , regardless of their location . More simply must be done to help prepare and support the hundreds of thousands of home owners at risk of flood . " <p> Public perception of flood risk <p> In spite of the fact that the country has seen increasing levels of rainfall and flooding in the past few years , a high percentage of the British public remain unaware of the dangers . A YouGov** survey published at the end of 2010 , identified some interesting public perceptions : <p> One in four British homes are at risk of flood yet 83 per cent of homeowners do not consider themselves to be at risk <p> 68 per cent of homeowners have put @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in the event of flooding <p> 95 per cent of those surveyed have contents insurance but 45 per cent do not know if it covers flooding <p> 43 per cent of British homeowners have not investigated the risk of flood to their current home <p> A mere 5 per cent of those surveyed stated that they plan to find out if they are at risk , while 48 per cent remain unsure if they will find out if a home they buy in future is under threat of flood . <p> **YouGov survey , October 2010 . Total sample size was 2378 adults . Fieldwork was undertaken between 13-15 October 2010 . The survey was carried out online . The figures have been weighted and are representative of all GB adults ( aged 18+ ) . <p> About the Know Your Flood Risk campaign <p> Know Your Flood Risk ( KYFR ) is a campaign founded following the devastating floods in 2007 . @@4365549 <h> Global warning : climate sceptics are winning the battle <p> Climate sceptics are winning the argument with the public over global warming , the world 's most celebrated climate scientist , James Hansen of NASA , said in London yesterday . <p> It is happening even though climate science itself is becoming ever clearer in showing that the earth is in increasing danger from rising temperatures , said Dr Hansen , who heads NASA 's Goddard Institute of Space Studies , and is widely thought of as " the father of global warming " -- his dramatic alert about climate change in US Senate hearings in July 1988 put the issue on the world agenda . <p> Since then he has been one of the most outspoken advocates of drastic climate action , and yesterday he also publicly criticised Germany 's recent decision to abandon its new nuclear power programme , formerly a key part of German climate measures , in the wake of the Fukushima nuclear disaster in Japan earlier this year . <p> " I think it was a big mistake , " he said @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Angela Merkel knows that , as she 's a physicist , but I think the political reality is she could n't stay in office if she expressed that opinion . " <p> In a briefing at the Royal Society , Dr Hansen , pictured , was frank about the success with public opinion of what he termed " the climate contrarians " , in effectively lessening public concern about global warming . He said : " They have been winning the argument for several years , even though the science has become clearer . <p> " There 's been a very strong campaign by those who want to continue fossil fuel ' business as usual ' , and the scientific story has not been powerful enough to offset that push . " <p> Part of the problem , he said , was that the climate sceptic lobby employed communications professionals , whereas " scientists are just barely competent at communicating with the public and do n't have the wherewithal to do it . " <p> The result was , he said , that in recent years " a gap has opened @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ scientific community , and what 's known by the people who need to know -- and that 's the public . " <p> Asked if anything might re-alert the public to the dangers of climate change , Dr Hansen said : " Mother Nature . " <p> Significant climatic " extreme events " were now occurring over 10 to 15 per cent of the planet annually , whereas between 1950 to 1980 they occurred over less than 1 per cent . He added : " So in places like Texas this year , Moscow last year , and Europe in 2003 , the climate change is so big that they are undeniable . Within 10 to 15 years they 're going to occur over 15 to 20 per cent of the planet , so people have to notice that the climate is changing . @@4367467 <h> 11 October 2011 2:07 PM <h> Why there is no British car industry <p> What on earth happened to the British car industry ? It is one of those astonishing , eye-popping facts that you have to pinch yourself every now and then to convince you that it is true , but it really is the case that in 2011 it is no longer possible to buy a family car made in Britain by a British-owned manufacturer . <p> In fact , aside from a few sporting oddities and bespoke one-offs , it is n't possible to buy ANY kind of car made in the UK by a UK firm . In 1950 the UK was the world 's LARGEST exporter of motor vehicles . Now wholly UK-owned firms , export just a handful of exotics . This is quite extraordinary and needs more explaining than the received wisdom usually on offer . <p> I 've been reading a fascinating book by motoring hack and auto-industry expert James Ruppert that explains , in grim detail , just what happened to an industry that used to comprise more @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ wares all over the world . <p> There are , in fact , several received wisdoms : The right tends to blame the unions , the left inept management . Everyone agrees our cars were rubbish compared to those made by the Germans , the Italians and , especially , the Japanese . British cars may have been all right in the 1950 , the thinking goes , but those dear old Wolseleys and MGs stood no chance against the Vorsprung durch Tecknikness of Volkswagen and Mighty Toyota . <p> There is a grain of truth in all of this , but only a grain and some of these assertions are plain wrong . For a start it is a myth that British cars were rubbish . We all remember the Allegros and Morris Marinas ( miserable machines ) but what about the original Mini ? The E-Type Jaguar ? The Morris Minor ? World-beaters , far superior to anything on offer from Germany , Italy or Japan . <p> It is a myth that British cars were all badly made . Part of this can be accounted for by the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ As Ruppert says , if you see a Lotus broken down by the roadside , people tend to snigger , make cruel remarks about British build quality and remind everyone that ' Lotus ' actually stands for ' Lots Of Trouble Usually Serious ' . But broken Ferraris are forgiven as temperamental Latin thoroughbreds . In the 1970s Fiats rusted as badly as Austins , and French cars even worse . You still see the odd Allegro trundling around but what happened to all those Renault 16s and Citroen Visas ? Iron oxide , each and every one of them . <p> It is a myth too that Britain could not make a car business work . Sadly , the most impressive example of a British motoring success story came not with a British firm but with a German one . Until I read Rupperts account , I did n't fully appreciate that the existence of the VAG behemoth , which today encompasses the Volkswagen , Audi , Seat , Skoda , Bentley and Bugatti brands is entirely the result of the work of a single British Army major , Ivan @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ after the war . <p> By the end of 1945 Maj. Hirst 's Volkswagen plant was churning out Kubelwagens ( a military version of the Beetle ) for the British Army and several thousand civilian cars for general sale . He galvanised the demoralised workforce and had the bomb-damaged factory repaired . <p> By 1946 Volkswagen was well on the way to becoming the world success story it remains to this day -- and it was in entirely British hands . So what happened ? The plant was offered for sale to Ford , which rejected it apparently on the grounds that Wolfsburg was too near Communist East Germany . The French did n't want it either and Hirst -- the man actually responsible for the success of the VW Beetle , the best-selling car in the world -- foiled an attempt to dismantle Volkswagen and ship the whole operation to Australia in lieu of war reparations . So surely the Brits would take it on , especially as we had one of our own men in charge ( and doing an excellent job ) ? <p> If things had gone @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Anglo-German venture . We would have seen Beetles being built in Essex , perhaps , Minis in Wolfsburg and a clever fusion of British flair and German technical brilliance . But of course this is not what happened . Instead , another Brit , Sir William Rootes visited the factory on behalf of the Government he declared that the Beetle was ' too ugly and noisy ' to sell . He told Hirst : " If you think you are going to build cars in this place , young man , you are a bloody fool ' . For a while Hirst was able to ignore him and VW went from strength to strength . But in the end control was handed back to the Germans and the rest is history . <p> The villains of the piece tend to be the grandees of the British Motor Industry , who often took the most perverse decisions imaginable . If Austin , Rover and the rest were blessed by the finest and most imaginative engineers and designers in the world , men like Alec Issigonis , David Bache and Spen King , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ who thought it was a good idea for Austin and Morris to sell almost identical cars in direct competition with each other despite being part of the same firm . <p> Yes , the unions did n't help . In the 1970s build quality was so bad on some BL products that their hapless first owners ( and the poor dealers ) often had to effectively rebuild them from the ground up . The Allegro was actually a pretty good car underneath , ruined by cost-cutting management , poor workmanship and a desire not to rock the boat with anything groundbreaking . <p> Another mistake was to locate car factories in places with no engineering tradition -- such as the decision to make Hillman Imps at a new plant in Scotland . The idea was to boost employment in deprived areas but the result was cars put together by inexperienced workers who really did not know what they were doing . <p> We can also blame Britain 's obsession with putting our national assets up for sale . The main reason France and Italy still have major indigenous car industries is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ million years countenance the sale of priceless companies such as Renault and Fiat to foreigners . <p> In the darkest days , these firms were protected by the state and allowed to flourish when times were better . We sold Austin Rover to the Germans , who in turn sold it to a bunch of venture capitalists who managed to turn a once-thriving British firm into a Chinese one , wrecking thousands of lives in the process and walking away with ? 40million , as the sacked Longbridge workers were given just ? 2,800 . <p> It 's a sorry tale . Of course people will insist we still DO have a car industry ; after all , we make more than a million vehicles a year on these shores . But these are not truly British cars . The profits from ' our ' car industry flow mostly back to shareholders in Germany , Japan and America . I know people who drive a Ford Mondeo seemingly unaware that their ' British ' car was made in Belgium or Germany by an American company . If you really want to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Morgan , for heaven 's sake . As I said , a sorry tale . <p> The British car Industry : Our Part in its Downfall , by James Ruppert , is published by Foresight Publications <h> Share this article : <h> Comments <p> You can follow this conversation by subscribing to the comment feed for this post . <p> How many times do you think Fiat ( Fix It Again Tony ) as been rescued by the Italian government.If Fiat ever went to the wall there 'd be a civil war in Italy.Same applies to Puegoet Citroen and Renault in France.Rover should have been rescued.The 75 was a beautiful car highley regarded in Italy the home of style . @@4367483 <h> British team head to Antarctic to drill into sub-glacial lake <p> A team of British researchers are preparing to dig down through three-kilometre-thick ice to sample a lake under the Antarctic in the hope of finding new species and clues about the future impact of climate change . <p> A team of engineers with 70 tonnes of gear are to head for Lake Ellsworth in West Antarctica . The lake has been isolated from the outside world for at least 125,000 years -- but it could be as many as a million . It 's about 10km long and two to three kilometres wide . <p> The team 's mission is to prepare the way for the " deep-field " research mission that will take place in October 2012 . They will then use hot water to melt through 3,000 metres of ice in order to reach the lake , which remains liquid due to geothermal heat coming from inside the Earth . This technique has been used before in Antarctic experiments , but never this deep . The hot water drill will need to operate continuously @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the ice . <p> Engineer Andy Tait , from British Antarctic Survey , explains : " The design of the hot water drill is very straight-forward - very similar to the hot water you might use on a jet spray to clean a car . The nozzle delivers water at 2,000 psi and 90C which is needed to melt the ice to create the hole . " <p> Finding a hose long enough for the job was a big challenge . Tait explains : " Drilling such a long hole requires a long hose - this is particularly difficult to find companies capable of producing a continuous hose of 3.4 kilometres . The hose needs to be strong enough to support not only its weight , but the weight of the drill nozzle on the end of it . " <p> The -20C temperatures inside the borehole means that its diameter reduces by 0.6 cm per hour , as the water refreezes . This leaves a window of around 24 hours in which to conduct all of the experiments before the hole becomes too narrow . <p> Once it has penetrated @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and sediment . <p> A probe -- comprising of two pressure cases containing instrumentation and power and communications -- will be sent down the borehole . Water will be sampled and archived at predetermined intervals , with an onboard microprocessor and data logger running continuously . A separate instrument will be sent to the bottom of the lake to hammer into the sediment and collect samples . <p> The team will be looking for life in the sub-glacial lake . If they find it , it will be a significant discovery , because it will have survived under high pressure , in complete darkness and in isolation for up to a million years . <p> In addition to looking for life forms in the lake , they will be studying the potential future impact of climate change . If the West Antarctic Ice Sheet completely melts , it thought that it could raise sea levels globally by at least three metres -- and perhaps seven metres . However , that 's not expected to occur for several hundreds of years . <p> One of the main challenges has been building equipment @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Matt Mowlem from the National Oceanography Centre in Southampton , which was responsible for building many of the instruments , told the BBC : " This is an unknown environment -- we do n't know , for example , whether there will be dissolved gases in the water . So the water at its pressure of 300 atmospheres will be sampled . But when we pull the probe up and the flasks hit the cold air in the borehole , the water will try to freeze ; the pressure then increases to around 2,700 atmospheres , and that 's greater than anything experienced in ocean engineering . " <p> So far , no-one has been able to sample any sub-glacial lake in Antarctica . A Russian team was forced to delay a mission to drill into Lake Vostok earlier this year . Meanwhile an American team is preparing to drill into Lake Whillans . <p> One of the main challenges has been testing the drilling technology . Tait explains : " Making equipment that is going to be robust enough to work in the environment at -20C is certainly a challenge @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a system are n't designed for such low temperatures . Many of the companies that provide equipment wo n't provide guarantees . Cold testing is of great issue in order that we have the confidence that this equipment , having been transported half way round the world will work the first time that we use it . " 
@@4367644 <h> Food high prices and volatility to continue ; investment in agriculture critical , says UN <p> Food price volatility featuring high prices is likely to continue and possibly increase , making poor farmers , consumers and countries more vulnerable to poverty and food insecurity , the United Nations ' three Rome-based agencies said in the global hunger report published Monday . <p> Small , import-dependent countries , particularly in Africa , are especially at risk . Many of them still face severe problems following the world food and economic crises of 2006-2008 , the UN Food and Agriculture Organization ( FAO ) , the International Fund for Agricultural Development ( IFAD ) and the World Food Programme ( WFP ) said in " The State of Food Insecurity in the World 2011 " ( SOFI ) , an annual flagship report which they jointly produced this year . <p> Such crises , including in the Horn of Africa , " are challenging our efforts to achieve the Millennium Development Goal ( MDG ) of reducing the proportion of people who suffer from hunger by half in 2015 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Diouf of FAO , Kanayo F. Nwanze of IFAD and Josette Sheeran of WFP , warned in a preface to the report . <p> ? Governments must ensure that a transparent and predictable regulatory environment is in place , one that promotes private investment and increases farm productivity . We must reduce food waste in developed countries through education and policies , and reduce food losses in developing countries by boosting investment in the entire value chain , especially post-harvest processing . More sustainable management of our natural resources , forests and fisheries are critical for the food security of many of the poorest members of society , ? the three heads said . <p> This year 's report focuses on high and volatile food prices , identified as major contributing factors in food insecurity at global level and a source of grave concern to the international community . <p> ? Demand from consumers in rapidly growing economies will increase , the population continues to grow , and further growth in bio-fuels will place additional demands on the food system , " the report said . <p> Moreover , food price @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ linkages between agricultural and energy markets and more frequent extreme weather events . <p> Stronger economies and high food prices present incentives for increased long-term investment in the agricultural sector , which can contribute to improved food security in the long run . When farmers react to higher prices with increased production it is essential to build on their short-term response with increased investment in agriculture , with emphasis on initiatives that support smallholders , who are the main food producers in many parts of the developing world . <p> At the same time , targeted safety nets are crucial for alleviating food insecurity in the short term . They must be designed in advance in consultation with the most vulnerable people . <p> The report stresses that investment in agriculture remains critical to sustainable , long-term food security . Key areas where such investments should be directed are cost-effective irrigation , improved land-management practices and better seeds developed through agricultural research . That would help reduce the production risks facing farmers , especially smallholders , and mitigate price volatility . <p> Private initiatives by millions of farmers and rural entrepreneurs @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ prices have also provided incentives for increased investments by corporate investors ( including cross-border public and private entities ) in all parts of the agricultural value chain . It is important that all investment considers and respects the rights of all existing users of land and related natural resources , benefits local communities , promotes food security and environmental sustainability , and contributes to adaptation to and mitigation of climate change impacts . <p> Together with increased investments , greater policy predictability and general openness to trade are more effective than other strategies such as export bans , the report noted . Restrictive trade policies can protect domestic prices from international price swings , but such restrictions often also increase susceptibility to domestic production shocks , thus failing to reduce domestic price volatility . Restrictive trade policies also risk increasing volatility and prices on international markets . 
@@4367716 <h> As reform of the EU 's agriculture policy gets underway , Miles King of the Grassland Trust tells Matilda Lee how intensive farming has all but destroyed England 's ' rainforests ' and why it 's time we started asking for more from our farmers <p> Once upon a time , England 's grasslands were wildlife havens , major carbon storage banks , home to a multitude of butterflies and other species and an inspiration for writers and painters from Thomas Hardy and Shakespeare to David Hockney and John Constable . But the multi-coloured grass and wildflower-rich fields and meadows teeming with an unseen army of fungi supporting soil health and fertility , have largely been destroyed . <p> Today , grasslands , which cover over half the agricultural land in England , some five million hectares , are usually identified through the uniform bright green fields blanketing the landscape . ' In the last 70 years , grasslands have been commandeered for one singular purpose : to provide cheap food through modern agriculture , ' says Miles King , director of the Grasslands Trust ( pictured @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ per cent ( or 100,000 hectares ) of England 's lowland grasslands are still rich in wildlife , with most cultivated entirely with rye grass using artificial fertilisers and herbicides and damaged from long-term overgrazing . ' Grasslands need wild species of flower , moss , grass and fungi . The problem with intensively managed grasslands is there is no diversity , ' he says . ' Because of the inputs and fertilisers , the fungi are n't there , so they are nowhere near as good at sequestering carbon . ' <p> ' England 's rainforests ' <p> Grasslands have dominated the landscape since Neolithic farming settlements began to clear England 's native tree cover 6,000 years ago . There is a growing , if slow , recognition of the value of the various goods and services grasslands provide . The government 's first National Ecosystem Assessment , published earlier this year , details how , among other things , grasslands help mitigate climate change through carbon absorption , purify drinking water , and provide homes for pollinating insects , with the latter alone worth an estimated ? 440 million @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ well-managed semi-natural grassland the term used to describe grassland modified by human activity can store about 80 tonnes a hectare of carbon . If you take an intensively managed grassland and start managing it extensively , with low inputs and low-intensity grazing , it can sequester up to three tons of carbon a year , ' King says . This carbon storage capacity is on a par , he says , with broad-leafed woodland . Nature 's Tapestry , a in-depth report on the state of England 's grasslands by the Grasslands Trust says that the 1.45 million hectares of semi-improved grassland in England is estimated to store around 300 million tonnes of carbon , nearly as much as in the UK 's entire forest carbon store . ' Globally there is as much carbon stored in grasslands as in forests , ' says King . ' Recent research points to the more diverse in species , the better at storing carbon . It is a collaboration - the more plants and more fungi there are , the more effectively carbon is stored , ' he says . <p> Grasslands future @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ grasslands hangs on reforms to the EU 's common agricultural policy ( CAP ) , which is still the single biggest influence on farming practices in the country . Since the UK adopted the CAP on entering the European Community in 1973 , farmers have been incentivised to increase production , at the expense of the environment and wildlife . Subsequent CAP reforms in 1992 and 2003 have delinked subsidies from production and introduced incentives for farmers to protect and preserve the environment , albeit on a loose system of conditionalities . To date , however , the CAP 's environmental stewardship schemes have , ' largely failed , ' says King . The Grasslands Trust is gearing up for the next set of CAP reforms , which start tomorrow and are finalised in 2013 . <p> ' Trying to change agricultural policy is probably one of the most difficult things you could try to do . It means going up against very powerful agricultural lobbies , ' says King . The Grasslands Trust lobby both in the UK and in Brussels through the Wildlife and Countryside Link , a network @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in 1980 . ' After five years of entry-level environment schemes , what evidence that has been collected suggests there has been very little change in terms of increasing biodiversity , ' comments King . ' There is also no evidence , because there has been no monitoring , that this has stopped further biodiversity loss . Currently , there is not enough money for farmers to move up to more meaningful high-level stewardship schemes . I am not anti-farmer , ' he continues . ' 99 per cent of grasslands are managed by farmers . The question is how much we are trying to get out of them and what kind of farming we want . ' <p> The sentiments are shared with another veteran CAP reformer , Jack Thurston , co-founder of farmsubsidy.org , which campaigns for more transparency in farm subsidies . ' We need to assert our power in this social contract . As taxpayers , we provide the subsidies for farmers , so we need to demand more as regards public access to land , animal welfare and environmental stewardship .
